This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project 

to make the world's books discoverable online. 

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject 

to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books 

are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover. 

Marks, notations and other maiginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book's long journey from the 

publisher to a library and finally to you. 

Usage guidelines 

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing tliis resource, we liave taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. 
We also ask that you: 

+ Make non-commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for 
personal, non-commercial purposes. 

+ Refrain fivm automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attributionTht GoogXt "watermark" you see on each file is essential for in forming people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner 
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liabili^ can be quite severe. 

About Google Book Search

discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web

VI. TREFACE. 

Mr. McDonnell at the commencement of this celebrated match, 
laboured under the disadvantages naturally consequent upon meet- 
ing a player of so much greater experience than himself The 
daily opportunities of practice long enjoyed by M. de la Bour- 
donnais with the famous Des Chapelles, the worthy successor of 
Philidor, had given that finish to his play not to be acquired by 
any other means. In the favorite French opening, the King's 
Pawn one square game, comparatively but little understood in this 
country, Mr. McDonnell relied, I have heard our first players 
agree, too much, and persisted too long in a system he had found 
to answer, giving odds, though it proved inefficient against the 
well-analyzed combinations of the great French player. The latter 
has borne, however, honorable testimony to Mr. McDonnell's skill 
which was to be expected from his own high acquirements ; since 
M^ de la Bourdonnais,in his new Chess Magazine, the Palamedes,* 
describes Mr. McDonnell as having been with the single exception 
of M. des Chapelles ^^ Le plus habile amateur d'£checs qu'il a 
connu.'* I cannot dismiss the consideration of this interesting 
match by our £nglish champion with La Bourdonnaig, without 
adding that Mr. McDonnell many times expressed himself, not only 
in his own private circle, but in the public rooms of the West-^ 
minster Chess Club, as to its being his intention, at some future 
day, to print the whole of these games himself, with copious notes, 
not liking a mere selection. How unfortunate was it that this 
idea was not carried into effect. 

On the various opinions ventured by me in the shape of short 
notices respecting the players receiving the Knight and shorter 
odds, I have often compressed the spirit of observations made on 
the subject by Mr. McDonnell himself. I have made these remarks 



* The Palamedes is published in Paris on the 15th of each month, the 
snbscriptioD for the work is 25 fraDCs per annum ; four numbers have al- 
ready appeared, which have met with very great approbation. In additi(Hi 
to the well known talents of the Editors, MM. de la Bourdonnais and 
Mexy, several of the most eminent chess-players in the different capitals 
of Europe will be found as contributors to its pages. 



PREVACB. Til. 

with g^eat diffidence, not pretending myself to rank higher than 
a deroted lover of the game ; hut I have aimed at strict impartiality, 
and, should I give offence in the most trivial matter, am hefore- 
hand in this assurance of its heing unintentional. 

Such is the natural progress of players possessing a genius for 
the game, that several of the gentlemen here recorded as receiving 
the piece are now amongst our hest amateurs. By the advice of 
my friends, I mention no names ; hut to all gentlemen frequenting 
the Metropolitan Chess Cluhs, it will not he difficult to fill up the 
stars hetween the initials hy which every player is denoted. 

The greater part of these games were played during the last 
two years, when Mr. McDonnell was at the top of his strength; 
some of his earlier games have heen introduced when their merit 
warranted that distinction. Many that I had preserved have heen 
thrown out on the suggestion of several first-rate players heing 
lost on the part of Mr. McDonnell's opponent, through some gross 
error. 

In conclusion, I have merely to state that it would have heen 
foreign to the nature of this work to introduce details of the hio-^ 
graphy of Mr. McDonnell and his personal relations with society. 
He was the son of Dr. McDonnell, of Belfast, and for many years 
filled the honorable situation, in this metropolis, of Secretary to 
the Committee of West India Merchants. Mr. Alexander McDonnell 
was also the author of various works on political economy, dis- 
tinguished,, as might be expected, for their originality of thought 
and depth of research. 

WILLIAM GREENWOOD WALKER, 

Hon. Sec, to the WeMtmiruter Chen OUb* 
July, 1836.

BOOK II. 

THRBS GAMBB, PLATBB BY MR. M^D., BUNDFOtDBD. 

It is to be regretted that the following are the only blindfold 
games played by Mr. McDonnell, which have been preserved ; he 
was considered to play, without seeing the board, nearly within 
a pawn and move of his usual strength. 

The following games were contested, in the year 18 — , at a Club 
held at Mr. Lewis's house, in the presence of a number of the prin- 
cipal amateurs, to one of whom I am indebted for a copy of them. 



FIRST GAME. 

Mr. M'Donnell plays, without seeing the board, a- 
gainst Mr. W******. In this game no odds were given, 
and Mr. M'D. bad the first move. This game was 
played December 10, 1828.

GAMES BT' THE LATE HR. Iff^DOVHELL 



BOOK III. 

GAMES IN WHICH MB. MCDONNELL GIVES THE 0I>D8 OP THE 

KNIGHT. 

The games here given, contested by Mr. M^D. and Mr. M., 
were part of a series, including from one hundred and twenty to 
one hundred and thirty, played at the Westminster Chess Club during 
the year 1834. The result of this long match was a balance of 
three games in favour of Mr. y[*******^ Finding the odds latterly 
rather too much, shortly before his decease, Mr. M'D. comxaenced 
giving Mr. M. the pawn and three moves, which, in the opinion 
of the editor, would have led to an equal and interesting match. 
Mr. M^D. had also engaged to play some games with Mr. M. with- 
out further odds than those resulting ftrom his not seeing the board. 

It affords strong testimony in favor of Mr. M'D.'s preeminent 
skill, that Mr. M******* has been well known for the last twelve 
or fourteen years as one of the strongest players in the kingdom, 
not resident in London.

Mr. McDonnell resigned the game. 

Mr. G. W***** having won ten, of the last fourteen 
games, played at the Pawn and two with Mr. M'D., the 
parties had commenced playing at the Pawn and move, 
at the time of Mr. M'D.'s death. The Editor regrets 
that none of the Pawn and move games between Mr. 
M'D. and Mr. G. W*****, were preserved. 

Mr. G. W***** is well known as one of the chief 



108 



OAME8 BY THE LATB MR. M'DOKNBLL 



players in England, and is the Author of various popu- 
lar works on the game of Chess. It is generally ac- 
knowledged, that no individual of the present time has 
done so much to promote the extension of the science, 
in every way, as Mr, G. W*****.

Mr. N***** resigns. Another game was played at 
the same sitting, which was won by Mr. N. 

Mr. N***** is well known in the Chess world as 
the best player in the Nottingham Club, and decidedly 
rates as one of the most skilful amateurs not resident in 
the metropolis: these games were played on the occasion 
of Mr. N.'s visiting London in 1833.

124 GAMES BY THE LATE MR. MCDONNELL 



BOOK IV. 



EVEN GAMES. 



In the year 1831, Mr. McDonnell commenced a match with Mr. 
P***** of twenty-one games on even terms. The following five 
games only were played ; the first game was drawn ; and Mr. D. 
won three, and lost one, of the other four. 

The match excited great interest among the Chess circle. I am 
not acquainted with the reason why it was not played out. Mr. F. 
has the reputation of being the first amateur player of the present 
day, but does not put it to the test, in the way Mr. M'D. did.
